=== BUGGY CODE ===
class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True
        
        def isForbidden(s):
            t = trie
            counter = 0
            for c in s:
                if c not in t:
                    //return False
                t = t[c]
                counter += 1
                if "end" in t:
                    return 'counter
            return False
        
        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(word[i:j])
            if truc:
                j = i - truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res

```
class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        forbidden = set(forbidden)
        left = 0
        ans = 0
        for right in range(len(word)):
            for mid in range(right, max(right-10, left-1), -1):
                if word[mid:right+1] in forbidden:
                    left = mid + 1
                    break
            ans = max(ans, right-left+1)
        return ans
```

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True
        
        def isForbidden(s):
            t = trie
            counter = 0
            for c in s:
                if c not in t:
                    return False
                t = t[c]
                counter += 1
                if "end" in t:
                    return counter
            return False
        
        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(word[i:j])
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)

Option 1: Minimal fix:
```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def isForbidden(s: str):
            t = trie
            counter = 0
            for c in s:
                if c not in t:
                    return False
                t = t[c]
                counter += 1
                if "end" in t:
                    return counter
            return False

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(word[i:j])
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```
Will this pass? Let's test with examples and edge cases.

Now, is the algorithm with j = i + truc - 1 always equivalent to min_end? Let's prove more rigorously.

Let F be set of forbidden intervals [k, e] (inclusive) with start k and end e. For a left boundary i, a substring word[i:r] is valid iff for every forbidden interval with k >= i, r <= e? Wait if forbidden interval starts at k >= i, it is included if r > e (because substring positions i..r-1 includes k..e if r-1 >= e => r >= e+1). So valid iff r <= e for all such intervals. Thus max r = min_{k>=i} e, or n if none. Let M_i = min_{k>=i} e (with M_i=n if none). Then answer = max_i (M_i - i) if M_i >= i. Since e>=k>=i, M_i>=i. If no intervals, M_i=n.

Scanning i from n-1 down to 0, maintain j = M_{i+1}? Let's see. At start before processing i, j should equal M_{i+1}? At i=n-1, M_n = n (no starts >= n). j=n. After processing i, j should become M_i = min(M_{i+1}, min_{forbidden intervals starting at i} e). The code only considers forbidden intervals starting at i that are prefixes of word[i:j] (i.e., with e < j? Actually if e < j, the forbidden word is fully contained in word[i:j]; isForbidden returns length L = e-i+1, so e = i+L-1. If e >= j, then the forbidden interval extends beyond current j; since M_{i+1} <= j? Wait j = M_{i+1}. If e >= j, then min(M_{i+1}, e) = M_{i+1} = j, so no update needed. So checking only within word[i:j] suffices. If multiple forbidden intervals starting at i, the one with smallest e will be a prefix of word[i:j] if its e < j; if all e >= j, no update. isForbidden returns the smallest e among those with e < j? It scans left to right and returns at first end in trie, which is the shortest forbidden prefix, i.e., smallest e. So if truc, e = i+truc-1 < j, update j = e. If no truc, no forbidden prefix with e < j, so min e >= j, no update. Thus j becomes M_i. Then res = max(res, j-i). Good.

Need maybe use `List` type import. If include import, code:
```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        max_len = 0
        for f in forbidden:
            max_len = max(max_len, len(f))
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def isForbidden(start: int, end: int):
            t = trie
            limit = min(end, start + max_len)
            for pos in range(start, limit):
                c = word[pos]
                if c not in t:
                    return False
                t = t[c]
                if "end" in t:
                    return pos - start + 1
            return False

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(i, j)
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```

We argued no. But if forbidden empty? j=n. Good. If forbidden has empty string? max_len=0, trie root end. isForbidden returns False, so answer n, which might be wrong if empty forbidden considered. Now, let's think about if there is a known LeetCode solution using trie of forbidden words and scanning from right to left exactly:
```
def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
    trie = {}
    for f in forbidden:
        node = trie
        for ch in f:
            if ch not in node:
                node[ch] = {}
            node = node[ch]
        node['#'] = True
    
    def find(s):
        node = trie
        for i, ch in enumerate(s):
            if ch not in node:
                return False
            node = node[ch]
            if '#' in node:
                return i + 1
        return False
    
    ans = 0
    right = len(word)
    for left in range(len(word)-1, -1, -1):
        length = find(word[left:right])
        if length:
            right = left + length - 1
        ans = max(ans, right - left)
    return ans
```
Yes, this is a known solution. It uses slicing but accepted? Maybe because forbidden length <=10 and right-left maybe large; slicing O(n) but maybe still passes? Actually if no forbidden, slicing each time O(n^2) in Python, but LeetCode constraints n=1e5, would TLE. The known solution often uses `word[left:right]`? Let's check memory: There is a LeetCode 2781 solution with trie and `for i in range(n-1,-1,-1): k = find(word[i:j])`; maybe accepted because `find` stops at first forbidden and slicing copies up to j-i, worst-case O(n^2) if no forbidden. But maybe constraints n=1e5 and forbidden length <=10, but if no forbidden, slicing total ~n^2/2, too slow. However, LeetCode Python might still pass? 1e10 operations no. So optimized index version is better.

If a forbidden word is literally `"end"`, then building trie for f="end": root -> 'e' -> 'n' -> 'd', then at node for 'd', set `t["end"] = True`. That's fine: the key "end" is a marker at the node for 'd'. But what if a forbidden word is `"e"`? Then node for 'e' has `"end": True`. If another forbidden word is `"end"`, node for 'e' also has child `'n'`. No conflict because marker key "end" at node for 'e' and child key 'n' are different. The marker "end" is a key in the same dictionary as character keys. If a forbidden word is `"end"` and another forbidden word is `"e"`? The node for 'e' would have `"end": True` and child `'n'`. No conflict. If a forbidden word is `"end"` and another forbidden word is `"endd"`? Node for 'd' (first d) has `"end": True` and child `'d'`. No conflict. The only conflict would be if a forbidden word contains the substring "end" as a character? No, "end" is a key in the dictionary at a node, but character keys are single characters. Since "end" is a multi-character string, it cannot equal a single-character key. Wait but if a forbidden word is `"e"`? The node for 'e' has key `"end"` marker. If a forbidden word is `"end"`, the path goes root['e'] -> node. At root, key 'e' is a single char. At node for 'e', key 'n' is a single char. The marker key "end" is at node for 'e' if forbidden "e" exists, and also at node for 'd' if forbidden "end" exists. No conflict because "end" marker is not a child character; it's a boolean marker. But what if a forbidden word is `"end"` and we are at node for 'e', and we check `if "end" in t`? That would be true only if forbidden "e" exists, not "end". Good. So marker "end" is safe because character keys are single characters, and "end" is length 3. However, if a forbidden word is `"e"`, the node for 'e' has marker "end". Now, let's think about if the final code should be exactly:
```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        max_len = 0
        for f in forbidden:
            max_len = max(max_len, len(f))
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def isForbidden(start: int, end: int):
            t = trie
            limit = min(end, start + max_len)
            for pos in range(start, limit):
                c = word[pos]
                if c not in t:
                    return False
                t = t[c]
                if "end" in t:
                    return pos - start + 1
            return False

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(i, j)
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```
This is good.

If `truc` is false, no. If `truc` is true, `i + truc - 1 < j` because the forbidden word is fully contained in word[i:j]. Let's verify: `isForbidden` scans positions `range(start, limit)` where `limit = min(end, start + max_len)`. If it returns at pos, pos < limit <= end. So pos - start + 1 = truc <= end - start. Thus i + truc - 1 <= end - 1 = j - 1 < j. So assignment okay. Using min would set j to same. No need.

Now, let's think about if there is a hidden issue with `limit = min(end, start + max_len)` and `max_len` maybe 0, `range(start, start)` empty. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a hidden issue with the original code's `trie` if `forbidden` contains a word with uppercase letters; no.

Now, let's consider if the final code should be:
```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        max_len = 0
        for f in forbidden:
            max_len = max(max_len, len(f))
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def isForbidden(start, end):
            t = trie
            for pos in range(start, min(end, start + max_len)):
                c = word[pos]
                if c not in t:
                    return False
                t = t[c]
                if "end" in t:
                    return pos - start + 1
            return False

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(i, j)
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```
This is concise. Should we include type hints for nested function? Not necessary. If include, okay.

=== RAW ANSWER ===

But "only the fixed code" suggests no extra text. I'll output plain code. Need ensure code is correct.

Let's consider if we should include `from typing import List`. Yes.

Final code:

```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        max_len = 0
        for f in forbidden:
            max_len = max(max_len, len(f))
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def isForbidden(start: int, end: int):
            t = trie
            limit = min(end, start + max_len)
            for pos in range(start, limit):
                c = word[pos]
                if c not in t:
                    return False
                t = t[c]
                if "end" in t:
                    return pos - start + 1
            return False

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(i, j)
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```

Need maybe if `forbidden` empty, max_len=0, isForbidden limit=start, returns False. Good.

=== FIXED CODE ===
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        max_len = 0
        for f in forbidden:
            max_len = max(max_len, len(f))
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def isForbidden(start: int, end: int):
            t = trie
            limit = min(end, start + max_len)
            for pos in range(start, limit):
                c = word[pos]
                if c not in t:
                    return False
                t = t[c]
                if "end" in t:
                    return pos - start + 1
            return False

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(i, j)
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
